> So let me give you a concrete example. Today, let's say your friend cancels dinner on you, and you are feeling a little bit lonely. And so what do you do in that moment? You open up Facebook. And in that moment, the designers in the control room want to schedule exactly one thing, which is to maximize how much time you spend on the screen. Now, instead, imagine if those designers created a different timeline that was the easiest way, using all of their data, to actually help you get out with the people that you care about? Just think, alleviating all loneliness in society, if that was the timeline that Facebook wanted to make possible for people.

**Для Напарника:** Когда человек в плохом состоянии, кот предлагает маленькое действие в реальном мире (вода, пройтись), а не удерживает в приложении.



# 🎬 How Gameplay Loops Keep You Playing — Adam Millard - The Architect of Games


### Геймплейный цикл: повторяющиеся действия и состояния  ·  [01:03](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=63)
**Мысль:** Стабильная структура (старт, усиление, поражение, награда, новый забег) с новыми элементами каждый раз сохраняет свежесть и растит мастерство.

> Gameplay is structured in a familiar pattern. First, you start in hell with only some basic abilities to your name, then, collect a buch of powerups as you explore, gradually fighting tougher and tougher enemies until one of these stupid bomb guys gets you and sends you right back to your dad’s house. It’s here that you can have a look at all the new stuff your hard work has just unlocked and get ready to head out once again. You’ll perform this sequence of actions over and over again as you play and by adding new elements like different weapons or god powers into every run, but keeping this fundamental structure the same, Supergiant can keep things fresh, whilst still allowing you to build your mastery of the underlying systems over time. In game actual design terms used by actual game developers, which is to say not me this is what’s called a gameplay loop, a series of repeated actions or emotional states that serves as the foundation for everything you do in the game.

**Для Напарника:** Цикл Напарника «пришёл, выбрал шаг, сессия, остров вырос, вернулся» повторять с узнаваемой структурой и добавлять новое содержимое.


### Игра работает на нескольких циклах разной длины  ·  [02:13](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=133)
**Мысль:** Короткий core loop должен быть приятен сам по себе, так как повторяется сотни раз; Mario копировали ради структуры, но забывали сделать приятным бег и прыжок.

> Hades’ cycle of death and rebirth is far from its only gameplay loop,- in fact, most games operate using multiple loops of different lengths and scales with some being as short as seconds. For example,in the equally hellish DOOM, the main gameplay loop of entering a room, shooting bad guys until they’re all dead, grabbing whatever they dropped and zooming off to the next pack can resolve multiple times a minute and as anyone who’s played doom before will know, this particular sequence of events makes up most of your playtime. These quick, mechanically intensive loops that you spend a lot of time directly engaging with are often called core loops, which is a phrase you may have heard before. It’s very important to make sure a games foundational mechanical interactions feel fun to use, because you’ll be going through the motions of their core loops maybe hundreds of times in any given play session. A single platforming challenge with an approach, execution and recovery in Mario, is a core loop older than a significant percentage of people watching this video, and nintendo have famously always gone to great lengths to make sure that Mario is fun to simply move around, because that’s what you’ll spend 99% of your time doing. The different challenges themselves are just layers on top of the same loop, so if the underlying gameplay isn’t fun, you’ll get bored of the different mechanical gimmicks on display no matter how creative. This is why rubbish platformers back in the day that tried to copy Mario ended up failing, they copied the larger loops and overall structure, but didn’t spend enough time making the core loop of running and jumping any fun. Short and snappy core loops are what keep players engaged in the short term, and the inherent satisfaction of pulling each step off helps them to internalise the fundamental gameplay patterns that developers expect them to know off by heart.

**Для Напарника:** Короткий цикл «поставить шаг и запустить таймер» должен быть сам по себе приятным (быстро, отзывчиво), прежде чем нагружать надстройками.


### Длинные циклы дают причину вернуться  ·  [03:54](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=234)
**Мысль:** Длинные циклы (кампания, рост персонажа) мотивируют абстрактно, тайнами и ростом, и возвращают, когда сиюминутный азарт угас.

> However, for much larger, longer gameplay loops, the opposite is true. Because a single cycle might take hours to resolve, it’s much harder to directly tie it to the mechanics. Take the cycle of conquest in a strategy game like total war. First, you pick where you want to invade, muster your armies, duke it out with the enemy, either win or lose the war and then either cement your hold on your new territory or slink back home to regroup. You might only get through one or two of these loops in a given play session, and it’s completely disconnected from the moment-to-moment gameplay, which is mostly clicking through menus or telling your dudes which orcs to shoot. Longer gameplay loops are much more concerned with offering an abstract source of motivation and something to come back to, rather than immediate fun. Old-school JRPGs turn a lot of people off because their core loop, that being individual turns in a combat encounter are usually pretty boring. However, other people love RPGs because they place a massive emphasis on high-level meta gameplay loops, where you get to see your characters and the story evolve over tens of hours, and the smaller gameplay loops are just steps towards that eventual goal. By offering up plot mysteries and the opportunity to see character growth, games can not only keep you entertained, but also give you a goal to shoot for, and the need to experience the satisfying resolution to this loop is something that will bring you back to the game after the more immediate thrill of the gameplay has faded.

**Для Напарника:** Остров и ориентиры на горизонте — длинный цикл; усиливать ощущение «что-то растёт и скоро будет», не давя стрик-страхом.


### Нужен средний цикл между короткими и длинными  ·  [05:20](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=320)
**Мысль:** Если делать только короткие циклы, нет причины вернуться; только длинные, и награда слишком отложена. Лучшие игры строят структуру вокруг одного-двух средних циклов с ориентирами.

> It’s by learning why gameplay loops work, and how they relate to player motivation, that we can understand that the problem of unengaging games is actually a two-pronged issue. The more emphasis a game places on short gameplay loops, the more fun it is in the moment, but it’s going to be more difficult to give players a reason to come back. Games with very long gameplay loops that delay satisfaction, on the other hand need you to put a lot of effort in before you start having fun and so they end up in the I’ll get around to it one day pile forever. So, how do compelling games fix both of these problems at the same time, well, more often than not, that special breed of endlessly playable games organise their entire structure around one or occasionally two medium-length gameplay loops supported by longer and shorter ones. This ensures that gameplay has a predictable rhythm and structure whilst still giving players a nice amount of short term goals to direct them to where the fun stuff is. For all the hate I and other people have given them over the years, stuff like towers and bases to capture, overused though they may be, serve as anchors for the medium length gameplay loop of exploring a new area, conquering it, then moving on. Just imagine if breath of the wild had no shrines, or if far cry had no cult outposts, you’d have vast swathes of the game where there’s nothing to do but travel to the next story segment, and it’d be really hard to get into a nice consistent gameplay groove.

**Для Напарника:** Добавить средний слой над одной сессией: например «дневной» или «недельный» ориентир на острове, который заканчивается заметным событием.


### Свобода без структуры приводит к скуке и растерянности  ·  [06:41](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=401)
**Мысль:** Полностью открытый мир теряет игроков; разбивка на восемь залов и синдикаты даёт посильные шаги к большой цели.

> As much as people claim to want total freedom, the sheer directionlessness of a hypothetical open world pokemon game for example would more often than not lead to people getting lost, confused and critically bored before they’d completed the game. But, by splitting things up into 8 gym leaders and one or two crime syndicates, each containing a number of goes around the core battling loop, devs can ensure that their players don’t feel overwhelmed as they slowly progress towards completing the next big story loop,.

**Для Напарника:** Не давать пустой экран свободного выбора: предлагать один следующий шаг (очередь), дробя большую цель.


### Два связанных цикла и постоянный недобор до покупки  ·  [07:12](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=432)
**Мысль:** В Steamworld Dig 2 цикл «шахта и улучшения» построен так, что вам всегда чуть-чуть не хватает на всё желаемое, что рождает новую цель.

> A lot of games, rather than having a single main loop, have two main loops instead that each feed into each other. Steamworld dig 2 is a great example of this particular style, with the entire structure of the game being built around the dual loops of mining in the caves, and then upgrading back in town. Here’s how it works. As you adventure deeper and deeper into the mines of El Machino, mining minerals and killing baddies, eventually, your pack will fill up or your torch will run out, and you’ll be forced to return to the surface. This means you’re invariably going to have to say goodbye to some precious goodies or an unexplored part of the dungeon. Once you get to the surface, you can sell your haul to buy fancy new upgrades but things are balanced in such a way that you’ll never quiiite have enough to buy everything you want, giving you a new goal to shoot for as you drop back into the mines in search of that loot you abandoned before in a constant cycle of effort and rewards, and new opportunities.

**Для Напарника:** Награды острова могут давать лёгкое «ещё чуть-чуть до следующей постройки» без дефицита и без вины за недобор.


### Длина сессии определяет длину циклов  ·  [08:14](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=494)
**Мысль:** Нужно подгонять один большой цикл под длину сессии и вставлять несколько средних, чтобы каждая сессия давала удовлетворение и оставляла что-то на потом.

> Having an ideal session length in mind when developing a game can be very helpful for figuring out how long gameplay loops should be. Ideally, you want a play session to last for about as long as a single big, meta loop, and to get in a few rotations of medium length loops in along the way. By following this structure, each play session can feel like a satisfying experience, but there’s always something new to do for when you come back.

**Для Напарника:** Подогнать длительность Фокуса (25 мин) под один завершённый «акт» на острове, оставляя крючок на следующий визит.


### Pikmin: дневной ритм делает прогресс постоянным  ·  [08:42](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=522)
**Мысль:** Утро, день и вечер с жёстким расписанием дают предсказуемый ритм и ощущение продвижения; сессия вмещает пять-шесть дней.

> Pikmin is fantastic at providing a constant sense of progress contained within a rhythmic central gameplay loop. The strict schedule imposed by pikmin’s day-based structure is the key to it’s endlessly playable design: each morning you’ll start off by replenishing your forces from the previous day’s losses, the afternoon will be spent forging into new terrain, fighting bosses or finding loot, and the evening will be spent in a mad dash to bring all your ill gotten gains back to your ship before night falls and nocturnal predators devour any pikmin left outside. Your average play session of an hour or two is enough to get in maybe five or six days, which is about enough time to complete or at least fully explore a single area.

**Для Напарника:** Ритм дня (утро, день, вечер) уже есть в небе AppBackdrop; можно привязать к нему смысловой ритм шагов и итогов.


### Даже короткие сессии делят на фазы  ·  [09:13](https://youtu.be/Sk-nbAtIUko?t=553)
**Мысль:** Devil Daggers разбит на фазы по минуте, что структурирует хаос и облегчает видеть прогресс; Factorio паузируется вокруг технологических вех.

> As a second example, Factorio has a much longer session length of a few hours, and from experience, they’re usually paced around each technological milestone. Building up to unlocking the next type of research takes around an entire play session, with smaller objectives like researching other techs, developing production line expansions and alien genocide missions to keep you engaged along the way. Even games with tiny play lengths like Devil Daggers still follow this structure, each one to two minute long playthrough is split into distinct phases, you’ve got the intro with the squid towers, then you’ve got your first upgrade and the first spider, then the second upgrade and your first centipede, etcetera, this helps to ground what’s otherwise a very chaotic game with an intense core loop in some sort of structure, and makes marking your progress as well as creating goals for yourself much easier.

**Для Напарника:** Разбить 25-минутную сессию на ощутимые фазы (начало, середина, финиш) с мягкими репликами кота.



# 🎬 How Games Use Feedback Loops — Game Maker's Toolkit


### Положительные и отрицательные петли: успех усиливает успех, а Mario Kart выравнивает  ·  [00:27](https://youtu.be/H4kbJObhcHw?t=27)
**Мысль:** Положительные петли компаундируют победы и поражения; отрицательные гасят изменения и держат равновесие.

> Feedback loops are systems where the output is fed back into the system as an input. And when it comes to game design, this typically means that a player’s success or failure impacts the likelihood of future successes and failures. And feedback loops come in two delicious flavours - positive feedback loops, and negative feedback loops. Let’s start with positive loops. These reinforce successes with more successes, and compound failures with more failures, and I think a good example of the first one is the kill streak system in the Call of Duty games, especially the earlier titles like Modern Warfare. In those games, getting a handful of kills in a single life grants you special “kill streak” bonuses, which might mean putting enemy locations on your radar, or allowing you to call in an airstrike or an attack helicopter. Which ultimately means that those who are already doing well by racking up kills, are rewarded with the tools to do even better. This can have something of a snowball effect - a sort of “rich get richer” type outcome where successes are compounded. But, of course, positive feedback loops can also be about how losses lead to further losses. Like in chess, where losing a piece from the board means that you now have fewer tactical options which could lead to even more losses down the line. On the opposite side of the coin are negative feedback loops, which balance out successes and failures. And there’s one example everyone always uses when talking about this stuff - but that’s just because it’s the perfect one. it’s the item distribution in Mario Kart. So, players who manage to get to the front of the pack will find themselves getting rather weak items like green shells and banana peels. But those who are at the back of the crowd are handed really powerful weaponry like the bullet bill, the golden mushroom, and - of course - the infamous blue shell which specifically targets the person in first place. So winners are punished, and losers are rewarded. If positive feedback loops work to compound changes in a game state, negative feedback loops strive to always counteract those changes and keep things in equilibrium.

**Для Напарника:** Для Напарника важно: неудачный день не должен запускать «спираль смерти»; ставим мягкие компенсирующие петли.


### В Pyre проигрыш всё равно даёт опыт, нет перманентных потерь: ошибка — кочка, не точка невозврата  ·  [06:44](https://youtu.be/H4kbJObhcHw?t=404)
**Мысль:** Положительная петля без компенсатора ведёт к спирали неудач; парой с отрицательной петлёй (опыт за поражение, нет permadeath) игра остаётся в зоне вызова.

> But Pyre counteracts this issue, by pairing those positive feedback loops - with negative feedback loops. So, yes, losing matches sucks but - here’s loop number one - you still receive some experience points for failing. Meaning that you’re not punished too much for messing up. Also, unlike XCOM, there’s no permadeath so you’re not massively screwed for future matches. And then on the other side of things, counteracting the issue of overpowered troops, there’s the liberation system. You see, Pyre is set in a sort of grim purgatory called the Downside - and the entire point of the game is to win these special liberation rites - because the prize is that the team’s leader gets to leave the Downside, have their crimes forgotten, and return to the Commonwealth. And in the game, this means that at various intervals throughout the adventure, you permanently send away your most powerful soldiers, because you can only liberate those who are of a high enough rank So I guess Pyre does have permadeath... but it’s a good thing. And all this means that the resulting experience - at least for me - is that the game is always a close and interesting challenge. Mistakes became a road bump on the way to the conclusion, not a tipping point that could send me into an inescapable death spiral.

**Для Напарника:** Ключевой принцип проекта: сессия, которую бросили или недоделали, всё равно что-то даёт (опыт, тёплая реплика), а не обнуляет прогресс.


### Скрытые отрицательные петли дают противоречивые сигналы (скрытая сложность)  ·  [08:16](https://youtu.be/H4kbJObhcHw?t=496)
**Мысль:** Если сообщить, что игра облегчается при ошибках, игроки специально ошибаются; наказание за успех противоречит поощрению.

> One big issue with negative feedback loops is that they can send mixed signals. As we talked about in this episode, games are carefully designed to entice and encourage players towards a certain way of playing - so punishing someone for successfully playing the game completely goes against those encouragements. This is one reason why is was so important for Capcom to never reveal that Resident Evil 4 was using a dynamic difficulty system - ultimately, a negative feedback loop. Telling players that the game will get harder if they’re doing well, or easier if they’re struggling, is effectively telling good players to make mistakes.

**Для Напарника:** Не делать адаптивную сложность, которая карает за успех; награда за прогресс должна быть понятной.


### Развяжи награду за мастерство и прогрессию: лучшим игрокам — бонус-уровни, а не больше денег  ·  [11:43](https://youtu.be/H4kbJObhcHw?t=703)
**Мысль:** Награда, которая даёт сильным больше ресурсов, оставляет слабых без нужного; поэтому награду за успех сделать содержательной, а не механической выгодой.

> But sometimes it’s best to remove a feedback loop altogether. In Devil May Cry, getting a high score at the end of a level will grant a player more red orbs which are used to buy better weapons and health potions. This is a positive feedback loop that has the unfortunate effect of giving the best players a helping hand that they really don’t need, and means struggling players won’t have enough cash to get the items they require. It can be a good idea to simply decouple rewards for doing well, from the progression of the game. Donkey Kong has these challenging-to-collect KONG letters but grabbing them all doesn’t earn you coins or extra lives or anything like that: instead, you get extra difficult bonus levels, which is a suitable reward for players who are already proving themselves to be good at the game.

**Для Напарника:** Награды за длинные/сложные сессии — косметика и редкие находки, а не ускоритель роста острова.



# 🎬 How Idle Games Infected The Entire Games Industry — Shoobaloo


### Игра ради устранения необходимости играть: начальный труд как фундамент  ·  [02:24](https://youtu.be/ZjrVc_5QNKY?t=144)
**Мысль:** Ты думаешь, что играешь за деньги, но реально играешь, чтобы убрать необходимость играть. Начальное рутинное кликанье - основа дизайна.

> Here is the key. You thought you were playing to get more money. Sure, it started out that way, but now you're actually playing to remove the need to play at all. That's the real loop. That's the addiction. That initial tedious, repetitive clicking, it's actually fundamental to an idle game's design.

**Для Напарника:** Идея автоматизации усилия: со временем привычка и ритуал облегчаются, и остров помогает выстраивать их.


### Лучший момент: ручное действие, которое наконец автоматизировалось  ·  [03:05](https://youtu.be/ZjrVc_5QNKY?t=185)
**Мысль:** Самое сильное удовольствие - когда искусственное трение ручного клика уходит, и апгрейд начинает кликать сам. Эффект облегчения после усилий.

> It's second best because the best is when you experience the artificial friction of clicking an upgrade manually in an incremental game that finally gets automated away and starts clicking itself. GOD DAMN, BOY. MIGHT as well hook me up to a freaking dopamine IV, dude. At this point, you could just let the game run in the background, but you don't do that. And if you did, you might regret it. As soon as you have enough currency for the next upgrade that would speed up your progress even a little, not purchasing it means missing out. You'll still make progress, but not as quickly as you could. The fear of missing out on potential progress is one of the most powerful hooks idle games utilize.

**Для Напарника:** Момент облегчения как награда: после нескольких сессий шаг стартует легче, и Напарник это замечает. FOMO-крючок из этого отрывка не применять (стыд/тревога).


### Награждается снятие усилия, а хорошая игра вводит новые механики по ходу  ·  [04:36](https://youtu.be/ZjrVc_5QNKY?t=276)
**Мысль:** Игры вознаграждают устранение усилия, а удерживают тем, что в нескольких точках появляются новые механики, превышая естественные пределы.

> I realized it's not just rewarding progress, it's rewarding the removal of effort. But, that's only the beginning. After you click to produce paperclips for a while, you soon automate paperclip production. But, don't forget to optimize your profit, balancing of the paperclips with their price. But, then, once you've reached the natural limits of a paperclip business, the game introduces new mechanics. This is important. Every good incremental game has multiple points in which new mechanics are introduced.

**Для Напарника:** Периодически открывать на острове новые возможности (не только больше того же), чтобы петля не наскучивала.


### Число - слабое событие: визуализируй рост, чтобы не нужно было думать  ·  [07:19](https://youtu.be/ZjrVc_5QNKY?t=439)
**Мысль:** Изменение 10 млн -> 11 млн требует когнитивного перевода. Если каждое число представлено видом и звуком, а системы выражены физическим расширением (башня), мозг откликается сильнее.

> However, a number just changing from 10 million to 11 million is a relatively weak sensory event, which again requires the aforementioned cognitive translation. What if we could bypass the translation, aka the thinking, entirely? Not only that, but what if we could light up the brain in a similar, if not superior, fashion as to what occurs during music making? My friends, surely I tell you this, no longer are we bound by the cookie clicking laws of our Levitical past. The Norp Apologue does not come to abolish the laws, but to fulfill them. In the Norp Apologue, every number is visually and orally represented. Whether it be in the Norps crashing into the rock headfirst or blasting it with a Gatling gun from a hot air balloon, which showers the floor with corresponding shards. Even the buildings and upgrades you unlock as you progress through the game are visual representations of that progress. They are visualized numbers, and the number must go up. Similarly, in Tower Wizard, every new system is expressed as a physical expansion of the tower. You begin as a lowly wizard summoning spirits, but soon you ascend into a wizard god and crack open the gates of the universe. All of the systems that are introduced along the way work together cohesively to juice up the numbers to new heights. By visually representing the numbers ascending into the heavens, your brain lights up like a symphony.

**Для Напарника:** Рост показывать формой на острове (здание, дерево, свет), а не числом: счётчики минут и сессий вторичны к образу.


### Пассивный дофамин-капельница против активной петли: убрать ожидание  ·  [10:17](https://youtu.be/ZjrVc_5QNKY?t=617)
**Мысль:** В idle-играх ожидание и трение, потом награда; в активных инкрементальных ожидание заменено действием, трение сразу сменяется облегчением, что подталкивает к ещё одному забегу.

> From the perspective of neuroscience, active incrementals fundamentally alter the dopamine delivery system. In an idle game, your brain gets a slow, passive IV drip. you wait, you experience friction, and eventually you get rewarded. But an active incremental game replaces the waiting with action. No longer are you bound by the limits of time. You aren't waiting hours for grandma to bake some cookies. You are dying, upgrading, and starting a new run every several seconds. The friction is instantly followed by the alleviation of that friction over and over again in a hyper-condensed loop. "Just one more run," you whisper under your breath. "Just one more upgrade. Just one more spike in power." You need the numbers to go higher. Idle games remove effort, but active incremental games remove waiting.

**Для Напарника:** Короткие сессии с быстрым циклом усилие -> облегчение; но без давящей петли ещё один заход (не навязывать).



# 🎬 How to be great at systems thinking — Dive Club 🤿


### Локальное решение против примитива (пример с артефактами Claude)  ·  [03:11](https://youtu.be/MC5jAxh0ymA?t=191)
**Мысль:** Кнопка «свернуть» решает локальную проблему, а новая абстракция вроде артефактов растёт и решает будущие задачи; дизайнер должен увидеть и отстоять примитив.

> Best features are the ones that solve multiple problems at once that are simple abstractions that can grow into ones that solve future problems and so part of shaping strategy I think is proposing a solution that is not just let's take artifacts as an example because we just talked about it there is a user problem which is that it's hard to write documents with Claude because it takes up your entire screen you can't actually see claude's commentary Etc you could find 10 different solutions that would solve that problem some of those Solutions are the seeds of A New Concept a new abstraction a new thing that you can build on and I think it's designer jobs to see that and advocate for it so you know to paint stupid ends of the spectrum you could just have a collapse button that takes long documents and hides them in the chat that would be like a local solution to a problem but it is not a of compounding abstract concept that you can build off a primitive in the product.

**Для Напарника:** Остров как примитив прогресса может нести будущие механики (редкие находки, ориентиры), вместо отдельных счётчиков и значков.



# 🎬 How to Keep Players Engaged (Without Being Evil) — Game Maker's Toolkit


### Вовлечение, а не зависимость: без скиннеровских коробок и ежедневных наград  ·  [00:26](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=26)
**Мысль:** Ответственный дизайнер вовлекает интересным, а не психологическими уловками: ежедневные награды, распад ресурсов, избегание потерь он не использует.

> That’s the word i want to use here, by the way. I’m talking about engaging games, not addictive games - which I don’t think responsible designers should be trying to craft. So I’m not interested in games that are designed in such a way that players can’t stop themselves, and I won’t be talking about games that use psychological tricks like skinner boxes, daily rewards, resource decay, loss aversion, and the like. This isn’t that video. Instead, I want to talk about fun things that games can do to make you want to keep playing - but without completely hijacking your brain.

**Для Напарника:** Совпадает с духом Напарника (без стыда и вины): не вводить стрик-штрафы и ежедневные принуждения; привлекать новизной и заслуженными находками.


### Темп: чередуй типы активности и интенсивность  ·  [01:04](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=64)
**Мысль:** Смена «столпов» геймплея спасает от скуки, а переключение между спокойным и интенсивным — от усталости и десенсибилизации; долго держать максимум нельзя.

> A crucial factor is pacing, which describes the rhythm of the gameplay you’ll be experiencing and is paramount to making sure a game doesn’t become boring or repetitive. So if we take a game like Uncharted we’ll see that it has lots of different types of gameplay, which are often called pillars. That includes combat, climbing, puzzles, cinematic set-pieces, and even non-interactive stuff like cutscenes. What you’ll notice is that Naughty Dog swaps between these pillars constantly, never lingering on one type of gameplay for too long. And what that means is as soon as you start to get bored of, say, shooting enemies, the game will switch to something else entirely, and hopefully regain your attention. But you can also consider the intensity of each pillar, as a puzzle is often much calmer than a frenzied firefight. And it’s important to move fluidly between different intensities, as spending too long on calm gameplay can obviously be boring - but, at the same time, trying to keep the game at max intensity for too long will lead to exhaustion or desensitisation.

**Для Напарника:** Сессия Фокуса: чередовать рабочий отрезок и мягкие паузы с котом; финал сессии как пик, затем спокойный Мир.


### Постоянная новизна удерживает даже при однотипном действии  ·  [03:17](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=197)
**Мысль:** Марио почти весь про платформинг, но каждый уровень приносит новый способ играть, и игрок хочет увидеть следующий.

> Often it introduces entirely new ideas - whether that’s areas, mechanics, enemy types, and so on. The Mario games are wonderful at this, as you never know what to expect from stage to stage. So even though there’s not such a diversity in types of gameplay - it’s mostly just platforming - by consistently introducing novel new ways to play, the game really keeps players engaged and keen to see what’s in the next level.

**Для Напарника:** Остров: каждый новый ориентир с новым силуэтом и маленькой историей, чтобы однотипные сессии давали разное.


### Намёк на будущее: ожидание действует до самой награды  ·  [03:44](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=224)
**Мысль:** Дразнить предстоящим (запертая дверь, неизвестная способность) возбуждает ещё до выдачи нового, и позволяет реже выдавать новое.

> Novelty can work even better when paired with mystery, anticipation, and foreshadowing, which is when you tease a player about something new coming up so they just can’t stop playing until they see what it is. Take a game like The Witness, where you come out of the starting area and, if you’re like most players, you’ll quickly stumble upon this door which features a puzzle that is essentially impossible to solve with your current knowledge. It really sticks in your head, though, and you’ll need to know what’s behind that door. This sort of stuff can keep you playing for ages. Remember when you saw Sen’s Fortress in Dark Souls, and just knew you had to see what was behind that giant door? The mystery of what’s around the next corner is one key reason that makes the Souls games so damn hard to put down. Metroidvania games do this well, also, as a game like Hollow Knight quickly establishes that you will be getting interesting new powers, but it doesn’t tell you what they are. It just teases you with things in the environment that you can’t bypass yet, leaving you to anticipate what cool new ability you’ll be getting next. The thing about giving players new stuff is that each drop is an exciting motivation to stay engaged, but this burns out rather quickly and the player needs another one soon after. But if you tease the player, you can get them excited before you’ve even dropped the new content, which increases the amount of time you can go before dropping new stuff. It’s economical!

**Для Напарника:** Мир: показывать силуэт или тень следующего ориентира на гряде, чтобы манил к следующей сессии без давления.


### Долгая цель плюс короткие вехи (пример Stardew Valley)  ·  [05:27](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=327)
**Мысль:** Мечта о преобразованной ферме держит часы рутины, потому что есть самовыражение, множество коротких целей и стратегические решения.

> Now, one of the most compelling things a game can do is let you make progress towards a long-term goal. Give someone a level cap they can work towards, or a map full of collectibles they can find, and they can be stuck in for weeks. But let’s dig a bit deeper into that. So a game that does this really well is the charming farming sim Stardew Valley which comes with an implicit long-term goal - you start with a messy patch of land and no money, but in time you’ll have transformed the land into an incredible farm that makes you loads of cash. And it’s that dream that can sustain many hours of toil, hard work, and, to be honest, kinda repetitive gameplay. So why does it keep us going? Well, I think it’s cool that you can make your farm however you like. You get to express yourself, set your own goals, and build something you are really proud of. The game has lots of short term goals to keep you going. Smaller rewards like the community centre and important milestones like adding buildings or improving your house give you something immediate to shoot for on the way towards the longer term ambition. There’s also a huge element of planning, as you must think long term about decisions when it comes to different crops and animals, seasons, romantic partners, and so on. You’re not just trudging mindlessly towards the goal, but making strategic choices to help you get there faster.

**Для Напарника:** Рост острова как долгая цель, с маленькими вехами после каждой сессии; игрок сам выбирает, как строится остров.


### Положительная петля роста, автоматизация и фантазия о цели  ·  [06:51](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=411)
**Мысль:** Заработал, купил лучшее, заработал больше; оптимизация системы, которая начинает работать сама; предвкушение момента, когда достигнешь цели.

> There’s also the thrill of exponential growth. In the game you’ll make a bit of money, so you can buy more seeds and better tools. This lets you make more money, to buy even more stuff, so you can go on to make loads of money. This positive feedback loop is at the heart of lots of engaging games, such as looting monsters and upgrading your character in Monster Hunter. There’s also something really fun about optimising the system in Stardew Valley. You start out by watering every crop by hand, but eventually you’ll come to have sprinklers that do the hard work for you. A game that does this sort of thing wonderfully is Factorio, which tasks you with building factories. You start out by digging up resources manually, but eventually create machines to do it for you. And while those machines initially need to be fed fuel, you’ll eventually have them powered by natural resources that don’t run out. Maybe its just a thing that appeals to programmers, but the desire to create an optimised system that works on its own is very potent. One final reason we like long term goals is that players can fantasise about what it will be like when they finally reach that point. An example is a skill tree in a game, where the player isn’t just ticking off these skills because it’s something to do - but because they’re looking forward to the day when they can use all of these skills, to absolutely wreck shop and make light work of stuff they found difficult at the beginning.

**Для Напарника:** Остров: накопленные сессии заметно ускоряют рост (оживает больше), а визуализация будущей цели (силуэты) подпитывает предвкушение.


### Состояние потока и провал как норма: короткие попытки, ощущение роста  ·  [08:16](https://youtu.be/hbzGO_Qonu0?t=496)
**Мысль:** Поток возникает, когда вызов не слишком лёгкий и не слишком трудный; к провалам возвращаются, если попытки короткие, есть ощущение улучшения и следующая будет другой.

> Another huge motivator is a compelling challenge. A game that is constantly pushing you to show your mastery of the game, in varied and interesting situations, can really keep you going. This requires pitch-perfect difficulty, though. As we talked about in the Resident Evil 4 episode, players only get into an engaging flow state when they meet a challenge that isn’t so easy as to be boring, but isn’t so difficult as to be stressful. Resident Evil uses dynamic difficulty to keep players in that sweet spot, but for most games, a finely tuned and fiercely play-tested difficulty curve is the way to go. Remember, though, that failure isn’t a bad thing. Some of the most engaging games like Tetris and Spelunky are filled with failure, but people will keep coming back if the runs are relatively short, if they feel a sense of getting better each time, and if they know the next session will be markedly different from the one before - often due to random generation of some kind.

**Для Напарника:** Дробление задачи на микрошаги и короткие сессии: неудача сессии не штраф, а следующий шаг выглядит иначе; тон без вины.



# 🎬 How to Make Learning as Addictive as Social Media | Duolingo's Luis Von Ahn | TED — TED


### Сделать «брокколи» на вкус как десерт: те же приёмы, что у соцсетей  ·  [05:12](https://youtu.be/P6FORpg0KVo?t=312)
**Мысль:** Смартфон конкурирует с TikTok и играми; чтобы люди хотели учиться, нужны те же психологические приёмы вовлечения, но направленные на полезное.

> However, if you're trying to deliver education with a smartphone, you run into a humongous problem. And it is that smartphones come equipped with some of the most addictive drugs that humanity has ever engineered. TikTok, Instagram, mobile games. See, delivering education over a smartphone is like hoping that people will eat their broccoli, but right next to it, you put the most delicious dessert ever made. (Laughter) If you really want to deliver education to everyone, not only do you have to make it accessible, but also you have to make it so that people want to actually learn. And with Duolingo, we've been able to do this. And at the highest level, the way we've done this is by making the broccoli taste like dessert. I'll say it another way. What we've done is that we've used the same psychological techniques that apps like Instagram, TikTok or mobile games use to keep people engaged, but in this case, we use them to keep people engaged but with education.

**Для Напарника:** Напарник применяет дофаминовые приёмы (награды, неожиданные находки, рост острова) ради фокуса, но без тёмных паттернов.



# 🎬 How to MAKE YOUR GAME LOOK GOOD! — Brackeys


### Свет ведёт игрока, а движение показывает, что объект интерактивен  ·  [05:00](https://youtu.be/nvbQ9_bzx1k?t=300)
**Мысль:** Игроки идут к свету, а на статичной сцене любое медленное движение сразу маркирует интерактивные предметы. Свет рассказывает маленькую историю о том, откуда он падает.

> Let's try adding some lighting to the scene. I find that when working with lighting, it helps if I think of it like I'm creating a narrative. In this case, we could imagine that we just wandered into this cave, and now some light is spilling in from the entrance. So I'll add light that shines some light from the entrance onto the box. But the scene still feels a bit unbalanced. There's a lot going on the left side with the contrast and lighting. So I'll add a dim light on the right to balance things out a bit. This also guides the player towards the exit of the level. And this is definitely a key takeaway. Players always go towards the light. Of course, right now the coins are looking a bit off. At first glance, you might not even realise that there are coins you can pick up. So let's fix that. As I mentioned earlier, movement has a huge influence on focus, especially when everything else is static like in the scene. So I've gone ahead and made this little script that makes the coins float up and down. And just like that, they clearly stand out as something you can interact with.

**Для Напарника:** На острове и у костра в AppBackdrop интерактивные находки можно мягко подсвечивать и слегка покачивать (медленно, по правилам диегетичности), чтобы они читались как «можно взять».



# 🎬 How To Think Like A Game Designer — Game Maker's Toolkit


### Не копируй механику, не поняв, почему она работает  ·  [00:00](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=0)
**Мысль:** Автосейв по чекпоинтам работал в Call of Duty, но убил страх в Alien: Isolation: игроки без опаски бродили, зная, что потеряют пару минут.

> Okay, so during the development of Alien: Isolation, the game had a pretty basic save system. It would automatically cache your data whenever you tripped invisible checkpoints, which were dotted throughout each level. It was added because it was easy to implement, simple to understand - and, in truth, because that's how saving worked in pretty much every game. Design director Gary Napper said "like any development team, we all play a lot of games and each have our favourites. Often our decisions and choices are coloured by the games we play". But... as development went along, the team started to question if this save system was actually the right choice. You see, because players knew that if they died they'd only lose a couple minutes of progress, they would just waltz around the space station without a care in the world. Not ideal... in a horror game. *Alien scream* So while these checkpoints might have worked in Call of Duty and Bioshock - Napper says it was "not the right approach when making a game that is designed from its core to terrify and put people on edge". Because here's the thing. It's totally fine to take influence from other games. Many designers borrow mechanics from their favourite titles - often evolving, combining, and remixing them into something new. But: it’s essential to understand why those mechanics work in one game, before copying and pasting them into yours.

**Для Напарника:** Перед заимствованием механики (стрик, сгорающий прогресс) проверять, какое поведение и чувство она даст в контексте СДВГ-продукта.


### MDA: механики, динамика, эстетика (чувства)  ·  [01:33](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=93)
**Мысль:** Механика (правила) порождает динамику (действия игрока) и эстетику (эмоции). Меняя число (запас патронов), каскадом меняешь поведение и чувства.

> Alright, so... way back in 2004, , three game designers got together and wrote an academic paper, featuring a framework that would prove to be perfect for analysing the mechanics in games. It's called MDA, and it breaks games down into three, neat sections: mechanics, dynamics, and aesthetics. Mechanics describes the actual workings of the game: like the rules, the systems, what the buttons do, and all of the individual stats and numbers. For example, one mechanic could be the maximum amount of ammo the player can hold - and we could set that number really high. Then, dynamics is how the player acts and behaves, in response to those mechanics. So because the player has tonnes of ammo, they may storm into battle, and shoot wildly in the general direction of every enemy they spot. And, finally, aesthetics - not to be confused with the graphics or the art style. Here, aesthetics is how the player feels when acting in that way. Their emotional response. So this wild shooting could make you feel reckless, powerful, unstoppable - basically, like this. *Machine gun fire* To put it another way - mechanics happen in the code, dynamics happen in the player's actions, and aesthetics happen in the player's feelings. Now, unfortunately, game designers can't just go inside the player's head and directly alter their actions or feelings. But they can change the code - and because MDA describes a casual relationship, these changes will cascade down and alter the dynamics and aesthetics. For example, if we tweak that knob the other way, and massively limit the player's ammo stash, that's definitely going to change the player's behaviour. Now, they will act cautiously before heading into battle, or might not engage enemies at all. Every shot fired will be done with careful consideration, and more time will be spent hunting for ammo in the environment. And this will lead to completely different aesthetics: like disempowerment, fear, and caution. So, this MDA framework takes mechanics - and then puts them into a wider context, which asks 'how do they make the player act, and how do they make the player feel?'.

**Для Напарника:** Анализировать каждую механику Напарника по цепочке: правило, что делает человек, что он при этом чувствует (облегчение, гордость, давление).


### Flower: убрали фичи, мешавшие спокойствию  ·  [05:04](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=304)
**Мысль:** Уровни, заклинания, ресурсы и таймеры вырезали, потому что они шли против намеченных эмоций покоя. Привычная «веселость» не всегда помогает нужному чувству.

> So MDA lets us see game mechanics as powerful vessels for delivering emotions to players. And so when borrowing mechanics from other games - or making entirely new ones - you can pick ones with associated dynamics and aesthetics that will compliment the rest of your design - and avoid ones that will clash. For instance, designer Jenova Chen says that the game Flower once had a level-up system, spells you could cast, resource management, and time limitations - expected features of other video games. But they all had to go, because they went against the intended emotions of relaxation, calm, and peace. Chen says "we’ve played so many games growing up these bad habits form. A lot of the time we like to make things very fun, but fun doesn’t always help the emotion you want to deliver".

**Для Напарника:** Для Напарника, чья эмоция тепло и присутствие, вычёркивать геймификацию, создающую гонку и тревогу (лидерборды, таймеры-давилки).


### Одна фраза-видение как маяк для решений  ·  [05:52](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=352)
**Мысль:** Subnautica: «thrill of the unknown», RE Village: «struggle to survive». Короткая формулировка позволяет оценивать и перебалансировать механики.

> That's why it's useful to truly understand your game's vision - the overarching feeling or experience you want to give the player. Because if you know you want to make the player feel, say, scared, then you know you'll want to pick mechanics that induce feelings of fear, dread, isolation, and disempowerment. That vision could be a single statement: Subnautica was based around the phrase "thrill of the unknown", and every mechanic had to suit this idea. Resident Evil Village was built under the banner of "struggle to survive" - and so when playtesters hated the game's messy combat, the designers could use that phrase as, like, a lodestar to rebalance and rethink the game's mechanics. The vision could also be a fantasy that you're trying to deliver. Perhaps you want the player to feel like Batman, or an assassin, or a world leader. It could be a feeling - like how Flower tried to make you feel relaxed. Or it could be a specific experience - when making FTL, the devs said their starting point was "we wanted to recreate the atmosphere of commanding a starship".

**Для Напарника:** Закрепить для Напарника фразу вроде «тёплое присутствие рядом, без вины» и сверять с ней каждую новую фичу.


### Игроки находят вырожденные стратегии  ·  [09:14](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=554)
**Мысль:** Предсказать поведение можно только плейтестом; игроки могут игнорировать механику или использовать её против эмоциональной цели (в Alien — бежать к чекпоинту и умирать).

> Also, while you can make best guesses about how a player will act in response to a mechanic, you can't know for sure until you do a lot of play-testing. Players might misunderstand or ignore a mechanic - or do something completely unexpected. Sometimes that's a fun, emergent behaviour - like the flying cars in Rocket League, which suited the vision just fine, and so could be kept in the game. But at other times it can create degenerate strategies that undermine your emotional goals. Going back to Alien: Isolation, the automatic checkpoints meant that if you got jumped by the Xenomorph, a smart strategy was to just beeline it towards to the next checkpoint, get killed, and respawn in safety.

**Для Напарника:** Проверять с реальными пользователями, не начнут ли они «ломать» механику награды (например, запускать пустые сессии ради острова).


### Сменили сейв — сменилась эмоция  ·  [10:58](https://youtu.be/iIOIT3dCy5w?t=658)
**Мысль:** Ручные сейвы с задержкой сделали сохранение напряжённым и усилили ужас. Одна механика полностью переписала поведение и чувства игроков.

> So, going back to Alien: Isolation - the team eventually tried a different save system. Something closer to older games. Now the game features a small number of save points that you have to operate manually. And - to make things worse - it takes a while for the save point to turn on, and for your game to actually save... giving the alien a chance to sneak up and kill you. And this new mechanic changed everything - completely altering the player's behaviours and emotions. "We were afraid," says Napper. "If we didn’t make it to the save point and successfully save, we would lose our progress. Saving became tense. Looking for a save became tense. The simple act of saving had become supportive to the game's driving factors of terror and isolation".

**Для Напарника:** Пример силы малой механики: сохранение и завершение сессии в Напарнике должны ощущаться как облегчение, а не как риск потери.



# 🎬 How Video Game Economies are Designed — Game Maker's Toolkit


### Источники ресурсов (taps) направляют поведение игрока и задают ценность  ·  [01:54](https://youtu.be/Zrf1cou_yVo?t=114)
**Мысль:** Куда и сколько падает ресурса — это стимул (лут с врагов, материалы по биомам) и регулятор редкости.

> Taps generates new resources into the economy. That could be enemies who drop experience points when they die. A saw mill that harvests wood. A regenerating health bar. Or rocks that spit out iron ore. Some taps generate resources automatically, on a timer - others need to be mined manually, by the player. Now the thing about taps is that can be used to incentivise player behaviour. If you want players to fight monsters, then you can put loot drops on the enemies. If you want to push players to explore the world, then you might put unique crafting materials in every biome. And if you want players to punch demons in the face, then shower them with health points when they glory kill baddies. Also, just like a real tap… you can change the flow of resources. This will affect how scarce, and valuable, a resource is: from rare ammo drops in a survival horror game, to an abundance of munitions in a fast-paced shooter.

**Для Напарника:** Откуда на острове берутся элементы (сессии, находки) должно совпадать с нужным поведением: фокус, а не накликивание.


### Положительная петля «повтори, стань сильнее» опасно затягивает и превращается в гринд  ·  [06:23](https://youtu.be/Zrf1cou_yVo?t=383)
**Мысль:** Идле-игры строятся на петле, которая подкрепляет саму себя, и это эффективно, но порой вредно.

> Idle games like Cookie Clicker, and roguelikes with permanent progression make players repeat the same, or very similar, content - but each time, with more power or efficiency than before. This creates a positive feedback loop - a system where the output is fed into the input, and then reinforces the output more strongly. And it can be very compelling - and dangerously addictive. But you know what else we might call repeating the same content to slowly amass power? Grinding. And that’s not always what we want.

**Для Напарника:** Не строить рост острова как гринд: ограничить накопление, чтобы мелкие сессии не копились ради очков.


### Отрицательная петля: стоимость повышения растёт вместе с уровнем и убивает фарм  ·  [09:03](https://youtu.be/Zrf1cou_yVo?t=543)
**Мысль:** В Elden Ring каждый следующий уровень требует всё больше рун, поэтому бить простых врагов неэффективно.

> A system that works to balance itself to a status quo. In. Elden Ring, if you graph out the number of runes needed to unlock the next level, then you’ll see that it makes a sharp, upward curve where it costs more and more experience points to jump to the next level. So while you only need to dispatch about 10 guards in Limgrave to jump from level 9 to level 10, you’d need to kill hundreds of them to go from level 49 to 50. By changing the cost of the conversion in lock step with your level, Elden Ring makes killing the same simple enemies over and over again incredibly ineffective - massively discouraging this tactic. And so players are pushed to explore further afield, fight tougher monsters, and take bigger risks.

**Для Напарника:** Рост острова не должен быть линейно зависим от числа коротких сессий; ценить длину и регулярность разумно.



# 🎬 I downloaded the Finch self care app! Here's how that's going. (Unsponsored review.) — Crash The Robot


### Неизвестный приз + привязка к трудной цели как дополнительный стимул  ·  [02:01](https://youtu.be/gEA7hxHfJ_8?t=121)
**Мысль:** Яйцо с микропитомцем вылупляется после 7 выполнений цели, неизвестно кто выйдет; можно привязать яйцо к цели, с которой борешься.

> It's not like B pushes me to buy them things I just find that the prospect of getting them cool new stuff is a very good motivator for getting things done here's another motivating thing about Finch sometimes BB finds an egg that will hatch into a micro pet for them after I've completed a particular goal 7 times there are a bunch of different micro pets to be had and I won't know what I'm going to get until I get those goals done and see the egg hatch so if I'm struggling with a particular goal I might link an egg to it in order to build some extra incentive.

**Для Напарника:** Редкие находки острова как переменная награда, связанная с конкретной сложной задачей; контингентность и неожиданность.



# 🎬 I Realized My Game’s Rewards Were Boring — RookieDev


### Два вида награды: делает сильнее и меняет, как ты думаешь  ·  [01:49](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=109)
**Мысль:** Запоминаются награды, меняющие взгляд на систему: перегрев из наказания превращается в тактическую возможность. Награда привязана к тому, что игрок сделал, поэтому её можно проследить.

> I started noticing that there are two very different kinds of reward in my game. Some rewards just make you stronger. Other rewards change what you're thinking about. And those second ones are the ones I remember. For example, Stellar Breaker has these things called discoveries. You get some of them from doing specific things in the game. Destroy Mercury and you can unlock Mercury core. That gives the cannon more damage while heat is low. Pretty simple. But I like that reward more than giving the player a random pile of currency because I can point at it and remember where it came from. I destroyed Mercury. Mercury gave me this thing. There's a little connection there. Then there's furnace seal. You get it from a major boss. Normally overheating your cannon is bad. You push too far, the cannon locks. Now you wait. Furnace seal gives your fleet extra damage while that lockout is happening. And that one I really like because now overheat isn't just a punishment anymore. Maybe I want to overheat. Maybe I push it on purpose right before a good damage window. The reward didn't just increase damage. It changed how I look at a system that was already there. That feels much better to me.

**Для Напарника:** Награды острова привязывать к конкретному поступку (закончил шаг на утреннюю тему -> появился такой-то объект), чтобы игрок помнил, откуда оно.


### Цепочка одинаковых множителей сливается в одну награду  ·  [03:31](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=211)
**Мысль:** Когда награды идут подряд и все дают просто процент, формально ты вознаграждён каждые 30 секунд, но ощущается всё как одно и то же.

> They have a job. The problem is when you get too many of them in a row. 10% more damage. 12% more damage. Another multiplier. another multiplier. At some point, you're technically being rewarded every 30 seconds, but it all starts feeling like the same reward.

**Для Напарника:** Не выдавать однотипные награды подряд (иконка, звёздочка): чередовать виды и размер, чтобы каждая ощущалась отдельным событием.


### Важная веха должна открывать возможность, а не ещё один процент  ·  [04:59](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=299)
**Мысль:** После 20 минут усилий хочется сказать Теперь я могу это, а не получить 1.15x. Простые числовые бонусы допустимы, но не на ключевых вехах.

> The problem is when every important milestone gives me another number. If I spend 20 minutes reaching something important, I want at least some of those moments to make me say, "Oh, I can do this now." Not, "Oh, 1.15x.".

**Для Напарника:** Вехи на острове (первая неделя, 10-я сессия) лучше выдавать как новое действие или существо, а не как счётчик.


### Ожидание важнее награды: неопределённость, предсказание и пауза-событие  ·  [05:21](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=321)
**Мысль:** Случайный мусор остаётся мусором: нужна желанная награда на другой стороне. Дофамин связан с предсказанием и ошибкой предсказания, поэтому момент перед наградой так же важен, а Vampire Survivors останавливает всё и превращает награду в событие.

> Expectation. If you already know exactly what you're getting every time, the reward can become very predictable. But if there's some uncertainty, you start anticipating it. Loot games use this constantly. Diablo is an obvious example. Something drops and for a second you don't know if it's trash or something that completely changes your build. That little moment matters. People sometimes reduce this to random rewards are addictive, which is technically touching part of the idea, but it's also not very useful as game design advice. Random garbage is still garbage. If the possible reward isn't interesting, making it unpredictable doesn't magically fix it. There has to be something on the other side that I actually want. That's also why I think people oversimplify dopamine so much. You hear dopamine is the pleasure chemical and then somehow every game design discussion becomes add lights, add sound, player gets dopamine. I don't think that's a useful way to think about it. A more useful idea is that reward learning has a lot to do with expectation. Your brain predicts something, then something actually happens and it learns from the difference. That's one reason the moment before a reward can sometimes be as important as the reward itself. Vampire Survivors is absurdly good at this. You pick up a chest. The game could just add the upgrade and keep going. Instead, everything stops. Music, lights, animation. You sit there waiting to see what comes out. Sometimes more things appear. Sometimes it keeps going longer than expected. The game turns a small mechanical reward into an event.

**Для Напарника:** Финал сессии (RevealIsland): короткая пауза-предвкушение перед тем, что выросло, вместо мгновенной выдачи; редкие находки как событие.


### Разная подача для обычных и редких наград, иначе ничто не выглядит важным  ·  [07:14](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=434)
**Мысль:** Если всё подаётся одинаково, игрок учится, что ничто не особенно важно. Обычное улучшение быстрое, открытие новой сущности и вехи заслуживают большей подачи.

> But right now, I think some rewards that should feel important don't feel different enough from the normal stuff. A normal upgrade can be fast. Click, number changes, done. But the first time you find a discovery, maybe that deserves more. A major ascension milestone, probably more. Something rare, definitely more. I need a clearer difference between, "Cool, you bought another level." And, "Wait, you just unlock something new." Because if everything gets the same presentation, the player learns that nothing is especially important.

**Для Напарника:** Ввести градацию подачи: обычная сессия - тихий рост, редкая находка или веха - полная хореография со звуком и хаптикой.


### Лучшие достижения влияют на игру ещё до получения  ·  [07:46](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=466)
**Мысль:** Достижения-челленджи заставляют придумывать, как выполнить условие, и тем самым уже меняют поведение, в отличие от простых отметок о достигнутой стадии.

> I think my achievements have the same split. Some of them are just milestones. Reach stage 500. Reach stage 750, reach stage 1,000. Those are fine. They're useful as markers, but they're not really the achievements I enjoy designing. There's one called vent timing. You have to use coolant vent when your cannon is almost overheated. That means you actually have to understand the heat system. Skeleton Crew asks you to beat a boss while leveling only a small number of ships. Frugal Ascent asks you to reach a certain point without buying run upgrades. Monod Doctrine pushes you toward getting almost all of your fleet damage from one class. Those are more fun to me because the achievement starts affecting you before you unlock it. You look at the requirement and think, "Okay, how am I going to do that?" Now, the reward has already changed how you're playing.

**Для Напарника:** Мягкие необязательные цели (например, начать шаг до 5 минут) как цель-приглашение, без стыда за невыполнение.


### Тест награды: изменила ли она то, чего игрок хочет дальше  ·  [08:55](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=535)
**Мысль:** В Balatro хороший джокер меняет, какие карты и руки ты ценишь, и покупки. Важные награды должны менять желания игрока.

> Another game that made me think about this is Bellatro. A good joker doesn't just make your score bigger. Sometimes it changes what cards you care about. It changes what hands you play. It changes what you buy. You start making decisions you weren't making before. That's a really nice test for a reward. Not every reward has to pass it, but I think the important ones should at least make me ask, did this change anything about what the player wants to do next?

**Для Напарника:** Вопрос-тест при проектировании каждой награды острова: что человек захочет сделать после неё?


### Увеличить анимацию не заменяет интересность самой награды  ·  [10:23](https://youtu.be/sW2U1_2_zNE?t=623)
**Мысль:** Психология награды не говорит, что должно волновать игрока. Вместо вопроса насколько большой награда, спрашивай, что игрок будет делать иначе. Запоминаются награды, дающие новую идею.

> But none of that can tell me what the player should care about. That's still my job. I can make the animation bigger. I can increase the drop rate. I can add another zero to the number. If the thing itself isn't interesting, I'm mostly decorating the problem. And I think that's the mistake I was making in parts of Stellar Breaker. I was asking how big should this reward be when sometimes I should have been asking what will the player do differently after getting it. Sometimes the answer can still be nothing. They just kill things faster. That's fine. But I don't want that to be every answer because I'm starting to think the rewards I remember most aren't always the biggest ones. They're the ones that give me a new idea.

**Для Напарника:** Не раздувать эффекты ради эффектов: интересность находки важнее размера салюта.



# 🎬 I Studied 500+ Gamified Apps (Here's What Actually Works) — Tim Gabe


### PBL-заблуждение: очки/бейджи/лидерборды — табло, а не игра  ·  [01:08](https://youtu.be/LXX_qOA5D8E?t=68)
**Мысль:** LinkedIn, Foursquare и Google News убрали бейджи: гонка за значком даёт количество вместо качества и действий, нужных бизнесу.

> Now, pattern one is what I call the PBL fallacy. PBL stands for points, badges, and leaderboards, the three mechanics every app reaches for first. And the reason this leads the video is because of what happened in 2024. LinkedIn quietly retired its community top voice gold badges. Their internal read was that badge-motivated users had been producing quantity over quality, chasing the badge instead of sharing expertise. And this isn't new. It's actually history repeating. Foursquare scrapped mayorships and badges in 2014 after their data showed gamification drove check-ins but not the discovery behavior that business actually needed. Google News killed its badge system for the same exact reason. The gamification OG, Yu-kai Chou, put it best. PBL is the scoreboard of a game, not the game itself. You wouldn't walk into a baseball stadium, look at the scoreboard, and feel motivated to play baseball, right? Most apps though build the scoreboard and forget to build the game.

**Для Напарника:** Остров — это «игра», а не табло: не строить ядро на очках и значках; рост мира должен отражать реальные действия.


### Переменная величина награды: предвкушение, раскрытие по одной, празднование (открытие паков)  ·  [07:29](https://youtu.be/LXX_qOA5D8E?t=449)
**Мысль:** Три стадии (ожидание, пошаговое раскрытие, празднование с хаптикой) превращают одно дофаминовое событие в пять; движущая сила — тяга вперёд, а не страх потери.

> Okay, so if streaks burn out at day 31, what works instead? Pattern five is variable reward magnitude, anticipation not loss aversion. The gap between knowing a reward is coming and not knowing how big it is. That's one of the strongest engagement signals in all of product design. Take the example of Gameblazers, a fantasy card game where the pack opening flow is the heart of the product. The product has three stages. Anticipation, you tap a pack, no idea what's inside, could be junk, could be the rare you've been chasing for weeks. Reveal, cards flip one at a time. Each card resets the anticipation cycle. Same data, but one dopamine event becomes five. And celebration, rare card hits, screen reacts, glows, haptics. That's why you tap the next pack. Now, compare that to a streak. Streaks run on fear of losing what you built. Variable rewards run on the pull towards what's next. Same surface behavior, opposite emotional engine. One burns out while the other keeps recharging itself.

**Для Напарника:** Reveal-хореография финала сессии (RevealIsland): ожидание, постепенное раскрытие находок, праздник с хаптикой; редкие находки как переменная величина.



# 🎬 I used AI to design my app's mascot (this is how) — Adam Lyttle


### Маскот должен иметь функцию в продукте, а не «приклеенную» роль  ·  [02:17](https://youtu.be/zmfzCi_fpJc?t=137)
**Мысль:** Идея пришла из вопроса, как маскот взаимодействует с пользователем: полосы на лбу как чёрные клавиши, белый кот как белые клавиши, спящий кот просыпается при ошибке.

> Then I had to think how this mascot would actually interact with the app itself so it doesn't just look like it's being plonked in there. If the identity of the app is going to be rebranded and based entirely around this mascot, I needed to serve an actual purpose for it so it actually fits in. So I started brainstorming some ideas. It's a piano app after all. So the mascot should have some simple element of music iconography. My original sketches revolved around this David Bowie inspired cat. But I just I wasn't connecting with it. I had to go back to the drawing board. I had to ask myself, how does this mascot interact with the user? How does this mascot make sense in the app? And that's when I had a bit of an aha moment. Instead of lightning bolt, why not make three stripes on the cat's forehead symbolic of the three black notes? From there, everything else just flowed really nicely. Instead of a gray cat, it's a white cat representing the white keys. And I felt like, hey, this is kind of a cool dude. He needs a little bow tie. And this is the first mascot that I've ever designed. I don't want to design something that's hard to animate. So, what's the easiest pose to animate? Well, if he's asleep the whole time, it's going to be really easy to animate. Then, when you hit a wrong note, the cat wakes up and you lose a life. This grounds the mascot into the app itself, and now it kind of makes sense.

**Для Напарника:** Роль кота в Напарнике (присутствие у костра, реакция на сессию) задавать как функцию продукта; без наказаний вроде потери жизни.



# 🎬 Idle Games: An Analysis — Game Soup


### Не обязательно «весело», чтобы быть затягивающим  ·  [01:27](https://youtu.be/2lEDS6Of1i4?t=87)
**Мысль:** Инкрементальные игры не очень веселы по механике, но очень притягательны: прогресс ради прогресса удерживает без эмерджентных взаимодействий.

> Clicker games are simultaneously some of the simplest games and some of the most addicting games I've ever played I'd hesitate to call Idol games fun because their mechanical structure necessarily foros the traditional player to game interactions that allow for an emergent experience but just because something isn't insanely fun doesn't mean that it can't be insanely compelling.

**Для Напарника:** Напарник может притягивать ощущением прогресса (остров растёт), не требуя развлекательной механики.



# 🎬 Idle Games: An Analysis — Game Soup Analysis


### Idle-игры менее веселы в минуту, но гораздо более затягивающие  ·  [02:21](https://youtu.be/SEBTKjD0x7M?t=141)
**Мысль:** Кликеры ускоряют прогресс эффективнее, чем игры с сюжетом, и требуют минимум навыка и внимания: чистая петля, морковка на палке.

### Цена ресурсов растёт быстрее богатства  ·  [05:16](https://youtu.be/2lEDS6Of1i4?t=316)

> At first glance most clicker games revolve around pumping up a single Resource as big as it can possibly be which you can then spend to increase the rate at which you gain that resource for example if you're buying buildings to produce cookies in cookie clicker each building will produce a static ount of cookies per second a cookie Farm gives eight cookies per second a factory will give 260 per second and a prism gives 2.9 billion cookies per second each of these buildings can be upgraded eight times and each upgrade doubles your production so the higher the base production rate the more effective the upgrade and it's extremely satisfying to watch your production rate Skyrocket after clicking your fingers off and it pulls you right back into the groove of things even if you're not necessarily within flow range and unlike a typical game where the mechanics get more and more challenging as you progress clickers make your actions less and less efficient instead with each new upgrade taking longer than the last so let's try to flesh out our previously one-dimensional summary of clickers and instead say this the cost of new resources gradually but inevitably outpaces your wealth accumulation that's clickers in a nutshell.

### Качественный успех: никто не говорит тебе, что ты провалился  ·  [07:40](https://youtu.be/2lEDS6Of1i4?t=460)
**Мысль:** В Dark Souls и Hearthstone игра считает ваши победы и поражения; в idle-играх нет условий победы или провала, и успех определяется субъективным восприятием прогресса.

> Important advantage to idle games that many modern AAA titles lack a subjective or qualitative success to give you an idea of what I mean by qualitative success let's look at the counterpart quantitative success if you die to an enemy ambush in Dark Souls you invoke a fail state which involves the game explicitly telling you that you died and you get sent back to the last bonfire at the end of your journey you can count up all the times you died and have a quantitative measure of all the times the game called your failures along the same lines when you defeat an opponent in Hearthstone this is an unqualified success a quantitative measure of your Victory the other guy of course gets a quantitative failure State when he loses but Idol games by contrast have no Victory or failure conditions you can't reach a point where your reflexes or logic aren't sufficient enough to continue likewise there's no amount of reflexes or logic that will get you to the end credits because they don't exist either so the barrier for entry is effectively zero but with all the Allure and satisfaction of sweet arbitrary progression and since games do lack Victory or failure conditions then whether you're succeeding or failing is entirely up to your own mind regardless of your actual efficiency your perception of progress in an idol game is unique to you and completely uninfluenced by those pesky quantitative measures that come from the game itself.

**Для Напарника:** Прямая поддержка принципа «без стыда»: не показывать счётчик провалов и невыполненного; прогресс определяется ощущением человека.

> Unlocks and new content can be mathematically locked behind days weeks or even months of play so there's always a long-term goal for the player to shoot for and the fact that a game can still surprise you after months plays heavily into what gives Idol games such a long tale I always look to a game called trimps here that exemplifies just how skillfully this can be done trims revolves around sending animals called trimps into battle you start in zone one and every Zone has 100 Battles but every battle is just a little bit more difficult than the last it's not uncommon to reach Zone 25 after a day or two of play but it can be weeks or even months before you reach Zone 230 which by the way unlocks a brand new game mechanic called magma this drastically changes the core gameplay altering the numerical Power Balance that you've become so familiar with up to this point and giving you something new to contend with and even the most efficient autoc clicking program won't be able to reach Zone to 230 in any meaningful amount of time to test this out I've had a game going for about a week now using an autor progression program called Auto trimps which makes the most efficient choices possible to progress I'm just now pushing into the lower 30 zones and it's only becoming more and more difficult from here but luckily every single zone carries the promise of something new like stronger equipment new jobs for your trimps or a brand new mechanic that revitalizes your interest.
